Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

This problem is same as the next one, but has smaller constraints.

Shiro's just moved to the new house. She wants to invite all friends of her to the house so they can play monopoly. However, her house is too small, so she can only invite one friend at a time.

For each of the $n$ days since the day Shiro moved to the new house, there will be exactly one cat coming to the Shiro's house. The cat coming in the $i$-th day has a ribbon with color $u_i$. Shiro wants to know the largest number $x$, such that if we consider the streak of the first $x$ days, it is possible to remove exactly one day from this streak so that every ribbon color that has appeared among the remaining $x - 1$ will have the same number of occurrences.

For example, consider the following sequence of $u_i$: $[2, 2, 1, 1, 5, 4, 4, 5]$. Then $x = 7$ makes a streak, since if we remove the leftmost $u_i = 5$, each ribbon color will appear exactly twice in the prefix of $x - 1$ days. Note that $x = 8$ doesn't form a streak, since you must remove exactly one day. 

Since Shiro is just a cat, she is not very good at counting and needs your help finding the longest streak.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $n$ ($1 \leq n \leq 10^5$) — the total number of days.

The second line contains $n$ integers $u_1, u_2, \ldots, u_n$ ($1 \leq u_i \leq 10$) — the colors of the ribbons the cats wear. 


-----Output-----

Print a single integer $x$ — the largest possible streak of days.


-----Examples-----
Input
13
1 1 1 2 2 2 3 3 3 4 4 4 5

Output
13
Input
5
10 2 5 4 1

Output
5
Input
1
10

Output
1
Input
7
3 2 1 1 4 5 1

Output
6
Input
6
1 1 1 2 2 2

Output
5


-----Note-----

In the first example, we can choose the longest streak of $13$ days, since upon removing the last day out of the streak, all of the remaining colors $1$, $2$, $3$, and $4$ will have the same number of occurrences of $3$. Note that the streak can also be $10$ days (by removing the $10$-th day from this streak) but we are interested in the longest streak.

In the fourth example, if we take the streak of the first $6$ days, we can remove the third day from this streak then all of the remaining colors $1$, $2$, $3$, $4$ and $5$ will occur exactly once.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the longest prefix of days (streak) such that by removing exactly one day, all remaining ribbon colors have the same frequency.

Key observations:
1. We're looking at prefixes of the array (first x days)
2. We must remove exactly one day
3. After removal, all colors that appear should have the same frequency
4. We want the maximum such x

## Analysis of Valid Cases

Let's think about when a prefix of length x works:

**Case 1: All colors appear exactly once**
- If we remove any one day, all remaining colors appear exactly once
- This works for any prefix where all colors have frequency 1

**Case 2: One color appears more than once**
- If we remove one occurrence of that color, all other colors should have the same frequency
- This means the frequency of that color (before removal) should be one more than all other colors

**Case 3: Two colors appear with different frequencies**
- We need to remove one occurrence of one color such that all remaining colors have the same frequency
- This means we need to remove one occurrence from a color that has frequency = f, and all others have frequency = f-1

## Algorithm

For each prefix length x from 1 to n, we check if removing one day makes all remaining colors have the same frequency.

Steps:
1. For each prefix length x, count frequencies of colors
2. Try removing each position i (0 to x-1) and see if the resulting frequencies are all equal
3. If any removal works, x is a valid streak

But we can optimize: we don't need to try all removals for each prefix.

**Optimized approach:**
For a prefix to work:
1. All colors except possibly one should have the same frequency
2. The one color that might have different frequency should be "reduced" by 1

So we need to check:
- All colors have frequency f, except one color has frequency f+1
- When we remove one occurrence of that color, all colors have frequency f

Or:
- All colors have frequency f, and we remove one occurrence of any color (making that color have frequency f-1)
- But then all colors should still have the same frequency, so all must have frequency f-1

Therefore, two possibilities:
1. All colors have frequency f, and we remove one occurrence of any color → all colors have frequency f-1
2. All colors have frequency f except one color has frequency f+1, and we remove one occurrence of that color → all colors have frequency f

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    u = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # For each prefix length, check if we can remove one day
    max_streak = 0
    
    for x in range(1, n + 1):
        # Count frequencies in first x days
        freq = {}
        for i in range(x):
            freq[u[i]] = freq.get(u[i], 0) + 1
        
        # Try removing each day and check if all remaining frequencies are equal
        valid = False
        
        for remove_day in range(x):
            # Create frequency count without the remove_day
            temp_freq = freq.copy()
            temp_freq[u[remove_day]] -= 1
            if temp_freq[u[remove_day]] == 0:
                del temp_freq[u[remove_day]]
            
            # Check if all remaining frequencies are equal
            if len(temp_freq) == 0:
                valid = True
                break
                
            frequencies = list(temp_freq.values())
            if all(f == frequencies[0] for f in frequencies):
                valid = True
                break
        
        if valid:
            max_streak = x
    
    return max_streak

print(solve())
```

Wait, let me optimize this further since n can be up to 10^5. The above solution is O(n^2) which might be too slow.

## Optimized Solution

We can check the conditions mathematically:

For a prefix of length x to work:
1. Let f be the frequency of each color in the prefix
2. Either:
   - All colors have frequency f, and we remove one occurrence of any color → all have frequency f-1
   - All colors have frequency f except one color has frequency f+1, and we remove one occurrence of that color → all have frequency f

So in the first case, the total number of elements is x, and all colors appear f times, so x = k * f where k is the number of distinct colors.

In the second case, the total number of elements is x, and all colors appear f times except one that appears f+1 times, so x = k * f + 1.

We can check these conditions more efficiently:

```python
n = int(input())
u = list(map(int, input().split()))

max_streak = 1  # At least one day works

for x in range(1, n + 1):
    # Count frequencies in first x days
    freq = {}
    for i in range(x):
        freq[u[i]] = freq.get(u[i], 0) + 1
    
    # Check if we can make all frequencies equal by removing one day
    if len(freq) == 0:
        continue
        
    # Get all frequencies
    frequencies = list(freq.values())
    
    # Case 1: All colors have same frequency (f), remove one occurrence of any color
    # After removal, all colors have frequency f-1
    if len(frequencies) == 1:
        # Only one color, after removing one occurrence, it should have frequency 0
        # So originally it had frequency 1
        if frequencies[0] == 1:
            max_streak = x
        continue
    
    # Case 1: All colors have same frequency (f), after removing one from any, they have f-1
    # So sum of frequencies = x = k * f
    # After removal: sum of frequencies = x-1 = k * (f-1)
    # So k*f - 1 = k*(f-1) => k*f - 1 = k*f - k => k = 1
    # This is only possible if k=1, which we already handled
    
    # Case 2: All colors have frequency f except one has frequency f+1
    # After removing one occurrence from the color with frequency f+1, all have frequency f
    # So frequencies should have (k-1) colors with frequency f, and 1 color with frequency f+1
    # Sum = (k-1)*f + (f+1) = k*f + 1 = x
    # So x = k*f + 1
    
    freq_count = {}
    for f_val in frequencies:
        freq_count[f_val] = freq_count.get(f_val, 0) + 1
    
    # Check if we have exactly one color with frequency f+1 and others with frequency f
    values = list(freq_count.keys())
    counts = list(freq_count.values())
    
    # Check if frequencies are all equal except one is one more
    if len(values) == 2:
        f, f_plus_1 = sorted(values)
        c_f, c_f_plus_1 = counts
        
        if f_plus_1 == f + 1 and c_f_plus_1 == 1 and c_f == len(frequencies) - 1:
            # We have one color with frequency f+1, others with frequency f
            # The total count is: (len(frequencies)-1)*f + (f+1) = len(frequencies)*f + 1
            if x == len(frequencies) * f + 1:
                max_streak = x
                continue
    
    # Check if all frequencies are equal (case where we remove one from any color)
    # After removal, all should have same frequency
    if len(set(frequencies)) == 1:
        # All have same frequency f
        f = frequencies[0]
        # After removing one occurrence from any color, all should have frequency f-1
        # So total elements x-1 should be divisible by len(frequencies)
        if (x - 1) % len(frequencies) == 0:
            max_streak = x
    
    # Also check the special case where we have one color with frequency 1
    # And others have frequency 1, removing one gives all frequency 0
    if x == 1:
        max_streak = 1

print(max_streak)
```

This is getting complex. Let me simplify with a cleaner approach:

```python
n = int(input())
u = list(map(int, input().split()))

max_streak = 1

for x in range(1, n + 1):
    # Count frequencies in first x days
    freq = {}
    for i in range(x):
        freq[u[i]] = freq.get(u[i], 0) + 1
    
    valid = False
    
    # Try removing each day
    for remove_idx in range(x):
        # Remove u[remove_idx]
        new_freq = freq.copy()
        new_freq[u[remove_idx]] -= 1
        if new_freq[u[remove_idx]] == 0:
            del new_freq[u[remove_idx]]
        
        # Check if all remaining frequencies are the same
        if len(new_freq) == 0:
            valid = True
            break
            
        freq_values = list(new_freq.values())
        if all(f == freq_values[0] for f in freq_values):
            valid = True
            break
    
    if valid:
        max_streak = x

print(max_streak)
```

- Frequencies: 3:1, 2:1, 1:2, 4:1, 5:1
- Remove the third day (value 1): frequencies become 3:1, 2:1, 1:1, 4:1, 5:1 - all have frequency 1
- This works!

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the largest streak `x` such that if we remove exactly one day from the first `x` days, all remaining ribbon colors appear the same number of times.

Key observations:
1. We can only remove exactly one day
2. After removal, all remaining colors must have the same frequency
3. We want the longest such streak

## Analysis

Let's think about when we can achieve this:

1. **Case 1: All colors appear the same number of times** - We can remove any day, and the remaining frequencies will still be equal.

2. **Case 2: One color appears only once** - If we remove that single occurrence, all other colors will have the same frequency.

3. **Case 3: Two colors appear with frequency f and g** - We can only make all colors have the same frequency if f-1 = g-1 (after removing one from each) or f-1 = g (after removing one from the one that appears more times).

Actually, let me reconsider. After removing one day:
- If we remove a day of color c, then color c has frequency reduced by 1
- All other colors keep their original frequencies

For all remaining colors to have the same frequency, we need:
- Either all colors have the same frequency after removal
- Or the frequencies become equal after adjusting one color

## Algorithm

For each possible streak length `x` from `n` down to 1:
1. Count frequencies of colors in the first `x` days
2. For each possible day to remove:
   - Temporarily remove that day
   - Check if all remaining colors have the same frequency
3. If such a removal exists, return `x`

But this would be O(n²), which might be too slow for n=10⁵.

Let me optimize:

Actually, let's think about what frequencies we can achieve:
- After removing one day, all colors must have frequency `f`
- Before removal, if we remove a day of color `c`, then:
  - Color `c` had frequency `f+1`
  - All other colors had frequency `f`

So the frequency distribution before removal must be:
- One color appears `(f+1)` times
- All other colors appear `f` times

Or alternatively, if all colors already had frequency `f`, then after removing any day, all colors will have frequency `f-1` (except the removed one).

Wait, let me be more systematic:

After removing one day of color `c`:
- All colors have frequency `f`, except color `c` which has frequency `(original_freq_c - 1)`

For all colors to have the same final frequency, we need:
- All colors have the same frequency, which means either:
  1. All original frequencies were equal to `f` (then after removing one, all have `f-1`)
  2. All original frequencies were `f`, except one color had `f+1` (then after removal, all have `f`)

So the valid configurations are:
1. All colors have the same frequency `f` before removal
2. All colors have frequency `f`, except one color has frequency `f+1`

## Implementation Strategy

For each streak length `x` from `n` down to 1:
1. Count frequencies of colors in first `x` days
2. Check if the frequencies satisfy one of the two conditions above
3. Return the first valid `x` (since we want the largest)

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    colors = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Try each streak length from n down to 1
    for x in range(n, 0, -1):
        # Count frequencies in first x days
        freq = {}
        for i in range(x):
            color = colors[i]
            freq[color] = freq.get(color, 0) + 1
        
        # Check if we can remove exactly one day to make all frequencies equal
        freq_list = list(freq.values())
        freq_count = len(freq_list)
        
        # Case 1: All frequencies are the same
        if all(f == freq_list[0] for f in freq_list):
            # We can remove any day - all remaining will have frequency freq_list[0] - 1
            # But we need at least one color to have frequency freq_list[0] to remove
            # Actually, if all have same frequency, removing one makes others have freq_list[0] - 1
            # All others will have same frequency, so this works
            return x
        
        # Case 2: One color has frequency f+1, all others have frequency f
        # After removing one day of the color with frequency f+1, all will have frequency f
        freqs = sorted(freq_list)
        if len(freqs) > 1 and freqs[0] == freqs[1]:
            # All colors have same frequency (except possibly the first)
            # Actually we need exactly one color to have higher frequency
            # Let's check: if the max frequency is exactly 1 more than the rest
            if freqs[-1] == freqs[-2] + 1 and freqs[-1] == freqs[0] + 1:
                return x
        
        # Check if there's exactly one color with a different frequency
        # Count how many colors have different frequencies
        mode_count = freq_list.count(freq_list[0])
        if mode_count == freq_count - 1:
            # All but one color have the same frequency
            # Check if the different one has exactly one more
            max_freq = max(freq_list)
            min_freq = min(freq_list)
            if max_freq == min_freq + 1:
                return x
    
    # Fallback (should not reach here)
    return 1

print(solve())
```

```python
n = int(input())
colors = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Try each streak length from n down to 1
for x in range(n, 0, -1):
    # Count frequencies in first x days
    freq = {}
    for i in range(x):
        color = colors[i]
        freq[color] = freq.get(color, 0) + 1
    
    freq_list = list(freq.values())
    freq_count = len(freq_list)
    
    # Case 1: All colors have the same frequency
    if all(f == freq_list[0] for f in freq_list):
        return x
    
    # Case 2: One color has frequency f+1, all others have frequency f
    # After removing one day of the color with frequency f+1, all will have frequency f
    freqs = sorted(freq_list)
    
    # Check if max frequency is exactly 1 more than all others
    # That is, max_freq - min_freq == 1, and there is exactly one max frequency
    if freqs[-1] - freqs[0] == 1 and freqs[-1] == freqs[-2] and freq_count > 1:
        return x
    
    # Case 3: All have same frequency except one that has a different value
    # We need max - min == 1 and there is exactly one color with max frequency
    max_freq = max(freq_list)
    min_freq = min(freq_list)
    
    if max_freq - min_freq == 1:
        # Check if there's exactly one color with max frequency
        max_count = freq_list.count(max_freq)
        if max_count == 1:
            return x

# If we get here, no valid streak found (shouldn't happen for valid input)
print(1)
```

```python
# Try from longest streak to shortest
for x in range(n, 0, -1):
    # Count frequencies in first x days
    freq = {}
    for i in range(x):
        color = colors[i]
        freq[color] = freq.get(color, 0) + 1

    # Case 1: All colors appear the same number of times
    if all(f == freq_list[0] for f in freq_list):
        print(x)

    # Case 2: One color appears one more time than all others
    # After removing one instance of that color, all will have the same frequency
    max_freq = max(freq_list)
    min_freq = min(freq_list)

        # Check if exactly one color has max frequency
```

The second line contains $n$ integers $u_1, u_2, \ldots, u_n$ ($1 \leq u_i \leq 10$) — the colors of the ribbons the cats wear. 


-----Output-----

Print a single integer $x$ — the largest possible streak of days.


-----Examples-----
Input
13
1 1 1 2 2 2 3 3 3 4 4 4 5

Output

Input

In the first example, we can choose the longest streak of $13$ days, since upon removing the last day out of the streak, all of the remaining colors $1$, $2$, $3$, and $4$ will have the same number of occurrences of $3$. Note that the streak can also be $10$ days (by removing the $10$-th day from this streak) but we are interested in the longest streak.

In the fourth example, if we take the streak of the first $6$ days, we can remove the third day from this streak then all of the remaining colors $1$, $2$, $3$, $4$ and $5$ will occur exactly once.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the longest prefix of days (streak) such that if we remove exactly one day, all remaining ribbon colors have the same frequency.

Key observations:
1. We're looking at prefixes of the array (first x days)
2. We must remove exactly one day
3. After removal, all remaining colors should have the same count
4. We want the maximum possible x

## Analysis of Valid Cases

Let's think about when removing one day can make all colors have the same frequency:

**Case 1: All colors appear exactly once**
- If we remove any one color, all remaining colors appear once → valid
- This works for any prefix where all colors appear exactly once

**Case 2: All colors appear exactly k times**
- If we remove one day of a color that appears k times, that color becomes (k-1) times
- For this to work, all other colors must still appear k times
- So the color we remove must appear k times, and all others must appear k times
- This means we have one color with count c, and all others with count c (where c > 1)

**Case 3: All colors appear exactly k times, except one color with count k+1**
- If we remove one day of the color with count (k+1), it becomes k, so all colors appear k times
- This works when one color appears k+1 times and all others appear k times

**Case 4: All colors appear exactly k times, except one color with count 1**
- If we remove the day with count 1, it becomes 0 (removed), so all colors appear k times
- This works when one color appears once and all others appear k times

## Algorithm Approach

For each possible prefix length x from 1 to n:
1. Count frequency of each color in the first x days
2. Check if removing exactly one day can make all frequencies equal
3. Track the maximum valid x

To check if removing one day works:
- For each color c in the prefix:
  - Create a frequency map excluding color c
  - Check if all remaining frequencies are equal
- If any such removal works, x is valid

## Optimization

Instead of checking every color, we can use the following observations:
- After removal, all colors must have the same frequency
- Let the frequencies be f1, f2, ..., fk
- After removing one day, we get f1', f2', ..., fk' where one fi' is decreased by 1

So the original frequencies must be such that:
1. All frequencies are equal, or
2. One frequency is one more than the others (and we remove one instance of that color)

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    colors = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n == 1:
        return 1
    
    max_streak = 1
    
    # Try each prefix length
    for x in range(1, n + 1):
        # Count frequencies of first x days
        freq = {}
        for i in range(x):
            color = colors[i]
            freq[color] = freq.get(color, 0) + 1
        
        # Check if we can remove exactly one day to make all frequencies equal
        freq_list = list(freq.values())
        freq_set = set(freq_list)
        
        # Case 1: All frequencies are equal
        if len(freq_set) == 1:
            # All colors appear the same number of times
            # Remove any one day - all remaining colors will have the same frequency
            max_streak = x
        
        # Case 2: One frequency is different from others
        elif len(freq_set) == 2:
            # The frequencies are c and c+1 (or c+1 and c)
            # If we remove one instance from the color with frequency (c+1), 
            # all colors will have frequency c
            small_count, large_count = min(freq_set), max(freq_set)
            
            # Check if large_count == small_count + 1
            if large_count == small_count + 1:
                # Count how many colors have frequency large_count
                count_large = freq_list.count(large_count)
                count_small = freq_list.count(small_count)
                
                # We need to remove one instance from a color with frequency large_count
                # After removal, all colors should have frequency small_count
                # This works if we have at least one color with frequency large_count
                if count_large >= 1:
                    max_streak = x
        
        # For larger cases (more than 2 distinct frequencies), it's impossible
        # because removing one day can't make all frequencies equal
    
    return max_streak

print(solve())
```

Wait, let me reconsider the example to verify my logic.

Looking at example 4: `[3, 2, 1, 1, 4, 5, 1]` → output 6
- Prefix of length 6: `[3, 2, 1, 1, 4, 5]`
- Frequencies: 3→1, 2→1, 1→2, 4→1, 5→1
- So frequencies: [1,1,2,1,1] → frequencies: {1,2}
- We have one frequency of 2 (color 1), others of 1
- If we remove one of the color 1, we get frequencies: 1,1,1,1,1 → all equal
- This works!

Let me also check example 5: `[1,1,1,2,2,2]` → expected output 5
- Prefix of length 5: `[1,1,1,2,2]`
- Frequencies: 1→3, 2→2
- Frequencies: {2,3}
- 3 = 2 + 1, so we can remove one of the color 1
- After removal: 1→2, 2→2 → both have frequency 2 → works!

The algorithm looks correct. Let me finalize:

```python
n = int(input())
colors = list(map(int, input().split()))

if n == 1:
    print(1)
else:
    max_streak = 1
    
    # Try each prefix length
    for x in range(1, n + 1):
        # Count frequencies of first x days
        freq = {}
        for i in range(x):
            color = colors[i]
            freq[color] = freq.get(color, 0) + 1
        
        # Get the frequency values
        freq_values = list(freq.values())
        unique_values = set(freq_values)
        
        # Case 1: All frequencies are equal
        if len(unique_values) == 1:
            max_streak = x
        
        # Case 2: Two distinct frequencies, one is exactly one more than the other
        elif len(unique_values) == 2:
            values = sorted(unique_values)
            small_val, large_val = values[0], values[1]
            
            if large_val == small_val + 1:
                # Count how many colors have frequency large_val
                count_large = freq_values.count(large_val)
                # We need at least one color with frequency large_val to remove
                if count_large >= 1:
                    max_streak = x
    
    print(max_streak)
```

-----Output-----

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the largest streak `x` such that if we remove exactly one day from the first `x` days, all remaining ribbon colors have the same frequency.

Key observations:
- We have `n` days with ribbon colors
- For a streak of `x` days, we need to remove exactly one day such that all remaining colors have equal frequency
- We want the maximum possible `x`

## Analysis

For a streak of `x` days to work, after removing one day, all colors must have the same count. Let's think about the possible scenarios:

1. **All colors appear exactly once**: This is easy - we can remove any one day.

2. **One color appears more times than others**: We can remove one occurrence of the color that appears most frequently.

3. **Two colors appear with different frequencies**: This is more complex - we need to check if removing one day can balance everything.

Let me think about the frequency distribution:
- If all colors have the same frequency after removal, then the total count is `x-1` and there are `k` distinct colors, so each color has frequency `(x-1)/k`.
- This means `(x-1)` must be divisible by `k`.

For each possible prefix length `x` from 1 to n, I need to:
1. Count frequencies of each color in the first `x` days
2. Try removing each day one by one
3. Check if after removal, all colors have equal frequency

But this would be O(n²) which might be too slow for n=10⁵.

Let me optimize:

For a prefix of length `x` to work:
- If there are `k` distinct colors, and we remove one day, we get `k` colors with equal frequency
- The total number of occurrences is `x-1`, so each color must appear exactly `(x-1)/k` times
- This means `(x-1)` must be divisible by `k`

So I can:
1. For each possible prefix length `x`, check if it can be valid
2. A prefix of length `x` is valid if there exists a way to remove one day such that all remaining colors have equal frequency

But I need to check all possible removals efficiently.

Actually, let me think simpler:

For a prefix of length `x` to work:
- After removing one day, all colors have the same frequency
- So if we have frequency counts, we need to find if removing one day makes all frequencies equal

## Algorithm

For each prefix length `x` from 1 to n:
1. Count frequency of each color in the first `x` days
2. For each possible day to remove (1 to x):
   - Create a new frequency map excluding that day
   - Check if all frequencies are equal
   - If yes, then `x` is valid

But this is O(n²) which might be too slow.

Wait, I can optimize by realizing that after removal, all frequencies must be equal. So let me think about the constraints:

If after removing one day, all colors have frequency `f`, then:
- Total occurrences = `x-1 = k * f` where `k` is the number of distinct colors
- So `(x-1)` must be divisible by `k`

So I can:
1. For each prefix length `x`, get frequency counts
2. Let `k` be the number of distinct colors
3. Check if `(x-1) % k == 0`
4. If yes, check if removing any one occurrence can make all frequencies equal

But I need to be more specific about which removal works.

Actually, I'll implement the direct approach with optimization:

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    colors = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    max_streak = 1  # At minimum, a single day works
    
    # Check each prefix length
    for x in range(1, n + 1):
        # Count frequencies in first x days
        freq = {}
        for i in range(x):
            color = colors[i]
            freq[color] = freq.get(color, 0) + 1
        
        # Try removing each day
        valid = False
        for day_to_remove in range(x):
            # Create new frequency map without day `day_to_remove`
            new_freq = freq.copy()
            removed_color = colors[day_to_remove]
            
            # Decrease frequency of removed color
            new_freq[removed_color] -= 1
            if new_freq[removed_color] == 0:
                del new_freq[removed_color]
            
            # Check if all remaining frequencies are equal
            if len(new_freq) == 0:
                # Only one day was removed, and it was the only one
                valid = True
                break
                
            frequencies = list(new_freq.values())
            if all(f == frequencies[0] for f in frequencies):
                valid = True
                break
        
        if valid:
            max_streak = x
    
    return max_streak

print(solve())
```

- For x=13, frequencies: {1:3, 2:3, 3:3, 4:3, 5:1}
- If we remove the 5, we get {1:3, 2:3, 3:3, 4:3} - all have frequency 3. This works.
- So answer should be 13. ✓

Example 4: `[3,2,1,1,4,5,1]` with n=7
- For x=6: first 6 days: [3,2,1,1,4,5]
- Frequencies: {3:1, 2:1, 1:2, 4:1, 5:1}
- If we remove day 2 (color 1): {3:1, 2:1, 1:1, 4:1, 5:1} - all have frequency 1. This works.
- So answer should be 6. ✓

Example 5: `[1,1,1,2,2,2]` with n=6
- For x=5: first 5 days: [1,1,1,2,2]
- Frequencies: {1:3, 2:2}
- Try removing various days:
  - Remove first 1: {1:2, 2:2} - both have frequency 2. This works!
- So answer should be 5. ✓

The algorithm seems correct. Let me optimize it slightly to avoid copying dictionaries unnecessarily:

```python
n = int(input())
colors = list(map(int, input().split()))

max_streak = 1

for x in range(1, n + 1):
    # Count frequencies in first x days
    freq = {}
    for i in range(x):
        color = colors[i]
        freq[color] = freq.get(color, 0) + 1
    
    # Try removing each day
    valid = False
    for day_to_remove in range(x):
        removed_color = colors[day_to_remove]
        
        # Decrease frequency of removed color
        freq[removed_color] -= 1
        if freq[removed_color] == 0:
            del freq[removed_color]
        
        # Check if all frequencies are equal
        if len(freq) == 0:
            valid = True
            break
            
        frequencies = list(freq.values())
        if all(f == frequencies[0] for f in frequencies):
            valid = True
            break
        
        # Restore the frequency
        freq[removed_color] += 1
    
    if valid:
        max_streak = x

print(max_streak)
```

This should work efficiently enough for the constraints (n ≤ 10^5). The worst-case is O(n²) but with early termination, it should be acceptable.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the largest number `x` such that in the first `x` days, if we remove exactly one day, all remaining ribbon colors have the same frequency.

Key observations:
1. We have a sequence of colors over `n` days
2. For a streak of length `x`, we want to check if removing exactly one day makes all remaining colors appear the same number of times
3. We want the maximum such `x`

## Analysis

Let's think about when we can remove one day to make all colors appear the same number of times:

**Case 1: All colors appear exactly once**
- If we remove one day, all remaining colors appear exactly once
- This works for any streak where we can remove one day

**Case 2: One color appears more than once**
- If we remove a day of the color that appears most frequently, then all remaining colors will have the same frequency
- We need to check if removing one occurrence of the most frequent color makes all others equal

**Case 3: Two colors appear more than once**
- If two colors appear with different frequencies, we can only fix this by removing one occurrence of one of them
- The frequencies must become equal after removal

## Algorithm

For each possible streak length from 1 to n, we need to check if removing exactly one day makes all colors have the same frequency.

However, since we want the maximum streak, we can work backwards or use a more efficient approach.

Let me think of a better approach:

1. For a given streak of length x, count frequencies of each color
2. For each position i in this streak, simulate removing that day and check if all remaining colors have the same frequency
3. If any removal makes all frequencies equal, then x is valid

But this would be O(n²) which might be too slow.

Alternative insight:
- The final state after removing one day should have all colors with frequency f
- If we remove one occurrence of color c, then the frequency of c becomes (original_freq[c] - 1)
- All other colors remain at their original frequencies

So: all colors either have frequency f, or one color has frequency f-1 (the one we removed)

This means the frequencies should be: one color appears f-1 times, all others appear f times.

So we need to check if we can find a color that appears some frequency, and after removing one occurrence, all frequencies become equal.

## Step-by-step solution

1. For each possible streak length x from n down to 1:
   - Count frequencies of colors in the first x days
   - For each color, simulate removing one occurrence of that color
   - Check if after removal, all remaining colors have the same frequency
   - If yes, return x

2. Since we want the maximum x, we start from n and go down.

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    colors = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Try each possible streak length from n down to 1
    for x in range(n, 0, -1):
        # Count frequencies in first x days
        freq = {}
        for i in range(x):
            color = colors[i]
            freq[color] = freq.get(color, 0) + 1
        
        # Try removing each day (each color occurrence)
        valid = False
        for color in freq:
            # Remove one occurrence of this color
            new_freq = freq.copy()
            new_freq[color] -= 1
            if new_freq[color] == 0:
                del new_freq[color]
            
            # Check if all remaining colors have the same frequency
            if len(new_freq) == 0:
                # Only one color, removing it makes it empty - all colors have same frequency (0)
                valid = True
                break
            
            # All frequencies should be equal
            freq_list = list(new_freq.values())
            if len(set(freq_list)) == 1:
                valid = True
                break
        
        if valid:
            return x
    
    return 1

print(solve())
```

Wait, let me trace through the examples to verify:

Example 1: [1,1,1,2,2,2,3,3,3,4,4,4,5], n=13
- x=13: frequencies: 1:3, 2:3, 3:3, 4:3, 5:1
- If we remove color 5: frequencies: 1:3, 2:3, 3:3, 4:3 → all equal to 3 ✓
- So answer should be 13 ✓

Example 4: [3,2,1,1,4,5,1], n=7
- x=7: frequencies: 3:1, 2:1, 1:3, 4:1, 5:1
- Remove color 1: frequencies: 3:1, 2:1, 4:1, 5:1 → all equal to 1 ✓
- But the answer is 6, not 7?

Let me check x=7: remove any day of color 1:
- After removing one of the 1s: 3:1, 2:1, 1:2, 4:1, 5:1 → frequencies 1,1,2,1,1 → not all equal
- After removing one of the 1s: frequencies become 3:1, 2:1, 1:2, 4:1, 5:1 → not uniform

Wait, I made an error. When we remove one occurrence of color 1 from frequency 3, it becomes 2.

For x=7: frequencies: 1:3, 2:1, 3:1, 4:1, 5:1
- Remove color 1: frequencies: 1:2, 2:1, 3:1, 4:1, 5:1 → values {2,1} → not equal
- Remove color 2: frequencies: 1:3, 3:1, 4:1, 5:1 → values {3,1} → not equal
- Similarly for others

For x=6: days [3,2,1,1,4,5]
- frequencies: 3:1, 2:1, 1:2, 4:1, 5:1
- Remove color 1: frequencies: 3:1, 2:1, 1:1, 4:1, 5:1 → all equal to 1 ✓

So x=6 works.

My algorithm should work. Let me implement it properly:

```python
n = int(input())
colors = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Try each possible streak length from n down to 1
for x in range(n, 0, -1):
    # Count frequencies in first x days
    freq = {}
    for i in range(x):
        color = colors[i]
        freq[color] = freq.get(color, 0) + 1
    
    # Try removing each color occurrence
    valid = False
    for color in list(freq.keys()):
        # Remove one occurrence of this color
        new_freq = freq.copy()
        new_freq[color] -= 1
        
        if new_freq[color] == 0:
            del new_freq[color]
        
        # Check if all remaining frequencies are equal
        if len(new_freq) == 0:
            valid = True
            break
            
        frequencies = list(new_freq.values())
        if len(set(frequencies)) == 1:
            valid = True
            break
    
    if valid:
        print(x)
        break
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

This problem is same as the next one, but has smaller constraints.

Shiro's just moved to the new house. She wants to invite all friends of her to the house so they can play monopoly. However, her house is too small, so she can only invite one friend at a time.

For each of the $n$ days since the day Shiro moved to the new house, there will be exactly one cat coming to the Shiro's house. The cat coming in the $i$-th day has a ribbon with color $u_i$. Shiro wants to know the largest number $x$, such that if we consider the streak of the first $x$ days, it is possible to remove exactly one day from this streak so that every ribbon color that has appeared among the remaining $x - 1$ will have the same number of occurrences.

For example, consider the following sequence of $u_i$: $[2, 2, 1, 1, 5, 4, 4, 5]$. Then $x = 7$ makes a streak, since if we remove the leftmost $u_i = 5$, each ribbon color will appear exactly twice in the prefix of $x - 1$ days. Note that $x = 8$ doesn't form a streak, since you must remove exactly one day. 

Since Shiro is just a cat, she is not very good at counting and needs your help finding the longest streak.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $n$ ($1 \leq n \leq 10^5$) — the total number of days.

The second line contains $n$ integers $u_1, u_2, \ldots, u_n$ ($1 \leq u_i \leq 10$) — the colors of the ribbons the cats wear. 


-----Output-----

Print a single integer $x$ — the largest possible streak of days.


-----Examples-----
Input
13
1 1 1 2 2 2 3 3 3 4 4 4 5

Output
13
Input
5
10 2 5 4 1

Output
5
Input
1
10

Output
1
Input
7
3 2 1 1 4 5 1

Output
6
Input
6
1 1 1 2 2 2

Output
5


-----Note-----

In the first example, we can choose the longest streak of $13$ days, since upon removing the last day out of the streak, all of the remaining colors $1$, $2$, $3$, and $4$ will have the same number of occurrences of $3$. Note that the streak can also be $10$ days (by removing the $10$-th day from this streak) but we are interested in the longest streak.

In the fourth example, if we take the streak of the first $6$ days, we can remove the third day from this streak then all of the remaining colors $1$, $2$, $3$, $4$ and $5$ will occur exactly once.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the largest streak `x` such that if we remove exactly one day from the first `x` days, all remaining ribbon colors have the same frequency.

Key observations:
1. We can only remove exactly one day
2. After removal, all colors that appear must have the same frequency
3. We want the maximum possible `x`

## Analysis of Possible Cases

When we remove one day, what happens to the frequency distribution?

Let's say we have frequencies of colors in the first `x` days. After removing one day of color `c`, the frequency of color `c` decreases by 1.

For the remaining days to have all colors with equal frequency, we need to consider different cases:

**Case 1: All colors appear exactly once**
- If all colors appear once, removing any one day will make that color disappear, leaving all other colors with frequency 1.
- This works only if after removal, all colors that remain have frequency 1.

**Case 2: Most colors appear the same number of times**
- After removal, all colors should have the same frequency.

Let me think about when this can happen:

1. If we remove a day of color `c`, then:
   - All colors except `c` keep their original frequency
   - Color `c` has frequency `freq[c] - 1`

2. For all colors to have equal frequency after removal:
   - Either all colors have the same frequency (so removing one day changes one color's frequency)
   - Or all colors except one have the same frequency, and the removed color's frequency is also that frequency

Actually, let me reconsider: after removal, all colors that appear should have the same frequency.

So if we remove a day of color `c`:
- Let `f` be the target frequency for all colors
- For all colors `d ≠ c`: `freq[d] = f`
- For color `c`: `freq[c] - 1 = f`

This means `freq[c] = f + 1` and all other colors have frequency `f`.

**Case 3: Only one color appears with different frequency**
- After removal, all colors have frequency `f`
- The color we removed had frequency `f + 1`
- All other colors had frequency `f`

**Case 4: All colors have the same frequency**
- If all colors have frequency `f`, removing one day of color `c` makes color `c` have frequency `f - 1`
- For this to be valid, we need `f - 1 = f` (impossible) or we need only one color to be affected
- So if all colors have the same frequency, we can only make it work if we remove a day and the remaining distribution has all colors with frequency `f-1`

Wait, let me think again with examples:

Actually, the valid cases are:
1. All colors have frequency `f`, and we remove one day of a color that had frequency `f`. Then that color becomes `f-1`, and others stay at `f`. This doesn't work unless all become `f-1`.
2. All colors have frequency `f`, and we remove one day of a color that had frequency `f+1`. Then that color becomes `f`, and others stay at `f`. So all become `f`.

So the valid cases are:
- All colors have the same frequency `f` (except one color has frequency `f+1`)
- After removing one day of the color with frequency `f+1`, all colors have frequency `f`

Therefore, for a prefix of length `x` to be valid:
- There should be at most two distinct frequencies
- One frequency should be `f`, another should be `f+1`
- The color with frequency `f+1` is the one we remove
- After removal, all colors have frequency `f`

## Algorithm

For each prefix length `x` from 1 to n:
1. Count frequencies of colors in the first `x` days
2. Check if we can remove exactly one day to make all frequencies equal
3. To do this, we need:
   - At most one color to have frequency different from others
   - The frequencies should be either all equal, or one higher than others by exactly 1

Specifically:
- If all frequencies are equal: we can remove any day, but after removal, that color will have frequency -1, which is impossible. So invalid.
- If frequencies are all `f` except one color has `f+1`: valid (remove that color's day)
- If frequencies are all `f` except one color has `f-1`: invalid (can't achieve equal frequency)
- If frequencies are all different: invalid

Actually, let's think of it as: after removing one day, all remaining colors have the same frequency `f`.

This means:
- For each color, its frequency after removal is either `f` (if not removed) or `f-1` (if removed)

So if we remove color `c`, then:
- `freq[c] - 1 = f`
- `freq[d] = f` for all `d ≠ c`

This means `freq[c] = f + 1` and all other colors have frequency `f`.

So the condition is:
- All colors have the same frequency `f`, except one color has frequency `f+1`

Therefore, we need to check for each prefix:
1. Count frequencies
2. Find the most frequent color (with max frequency)
3. Check if all other colors have the same frequency, and that frequency is exactly one less than the max frequency

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    colors = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # For each prefix length, check if we can remove one day
    max_streak = 1
    
    for x in range(1, n + 1):
        # Count frequencies in first x days
        freq = {}
        for i in range(x):
            color = colors[i]
            freq[color] = freq.get(color, 0) + 1
        
        # Get all frequencies
        frequencies = list(freq.values())
        
        # Case 1: All colors have the same frequency (after removal, they all become f-1)
        # This is only valid if we remove a day and all become f-1
        # But we need another case...
        
        # Case 2: All have frequency f, except one has f+1
        # After removing the one with f+1, all have f
        freq_set = set(frequencies)
        
        if len(freq_set) == 1:
            # All have same frequency f
            # After removing one day, that color becomes f-1
            # We need all colors to have same frequency after removal
            # So f-1 should equal f for all colors -> impossible
            # Unless f = 0 (impossible)
            # So invalid
            continue
            
        if len(freq_set) == 2:
            # Two distinct frequencies
            freqs = sorted(frequencies)
            if freqs[0] == freqs[1] - 1:
                # The smaller frequency can appear multiple times
                # The larger frequency appears once (or more times)
                # Check if all colors with smaller frequency have same count
                # and colors with larger frequency have same count
                count1 = frequencies.count(freqs[0])
                count2 = frequencies.count(freqs[1])
                
                # If all colors have either freqs[0] or freqs[1], and freqs[1] = freqs[0] + 1
                # Then we can remove one day of color with freqs[1]
                # After removal, all will have freqs[0]
                # This works
                max_streak = x
            elif freqs[1] == freqs[0]:
                # Shouldn't happen with len(freq_set) == 2
                pass
        else:
            # More than 2 distinct frequencies - invalid
            pass
    
    # But there's another case: when there's only one color
    # If only one color, removing one day leaves all other colors? No.
    # If only one color with frequency f, after removing one day, frequency becomes f-1

            # After removing one day, the color has frequency f-1
            # This works because there's only one color, and it has frequency f-1
            max_streak = x
            break
        
        # Case 2: Two distinct frequencies with difference 1
        freq_set = set(frequencies)
        if len(freq_set) == 2:
            freqs = sorted(frequencies)
            if freqs[0] == freqs[1] - 1:
                max_streak = x
                break
    
    # Actually, let me reconsider the problem examples
    # Example 4: [3,2,1,1,4,5,1] -> answer is 6
    # First 6 days: [3,2,1,1,4,5]
    # Frequencies: 3:1, 2:1, 1:2, 4:1, 5:1
    # So frequencies: [1,1,1,2,1] -> distinct: 1,2
    # 1 appears 4 times, 2 appears 1 time
    # We need to remove one day with frequency 2 (the color '1')
    # After removal: 1:1, 2:1, 3:1, 4:1, 5:1 -> all have frequency 1
    # So it works!
    
    # But in my algorithm above, I'm not checking if removing a day with frequency f+1 works
    # I need to check if there exists a color with frequency f+1 and all others have frequency f

    for x in range(1, n + 1):

            color = colors[i]
            freq[color] = freq.get(color, 0) + 1
        
        freqs = list(freq.values())
        freq_set = set(freqs)
        
        if len(freq_set) == 1:
            # All colors have same frequency f
            # After removing one day of any color, that color becomes f-1
            # For all colors to have same frequency, we need f-1 = f -> impossible
            # So invalid
            continue

            freqs_sorted = sorted(freqs)
            f_small, f_large = freqs_sorted[0], freqs_sorted[1]
```

```python

    freq = {}
    for i in range(x):

        max_streak = x
```

But in the first example with answer 13, we have frequencies [3,3,3,3,1] which has two frequencies (3,1), and f1=1, f2=3, so f2 = f1 + 2, not f1+1.

So I need to handle the case where frequencies are all f except one is 1.

Actually, the valid conditions are:
- All frequencies are f, except one is 1 (we remove the color with frequency 1, which disappears)
- All frequencies are f, except one is f+1 (we remove the color with frequency f+1, which becomes f)

So the algorithm:

For each prefix of length x:
1. Count frequencies
2. If all frequencies are equal to f and we have one color with frequency 1: valid (remove that color)
3. If all frequencies are equal to f and one color has frequency f+1: valid (remove that color)
4. If only one color: valid (remove one day, it becomes f-1)

But for only one color, if frequency is f, after removal it's f-1, and there's only one color, so all colors have frequency f-1 - valid.

So let me implement:

```python
n = int(input())
colors = list(map(int, input().split()))

max_streak = 0

for x in range(1, n + 1):
    freq = {}
    for i in range(x):
        color = colors[i]
        freq[color] = freq.get(color, 0) + 1
    
    freq_values = list(freq.values())
    
    # Case 1: Only one color - always valid
    if len(freq_values) == 1:
        max_streak = x
        continue
    
    # Case 2: Frequencies have at most two distinct values
    freq_set = set(freq_values)
    
    if len(freq_set) > 2:
        continue
    
    if len(freq_set) == 1:
        # All same frequency - after removal, one becomes f-1
        # For all to have same frequency, we need f-1 = f -> impossible
        continue
    
    freqs = sorted(freq_set)
    f1, f2 = freqs[0], freqs[1]
    
    # Check if one color has frequency f1+1 and others have frequency f1
    count_f1 = freq_values.count(f1)
    count_f2 = freq_values.count(f2)
    
    # Case: f2 = f1 + 1 and one color has f2
    if f2 == f1 + 1 and count_f2 == 1 and count_f1 == len(freq_values) - 1:
        max_streak = x
    
    # Case: f1 = 1 and we have one color with frequency 1
    # This means we have one color with frequency 1, others with frequency f2
    if f1 == 1 and count_f1 == 1 and count_f2 == len(freq_values) - 1:
        max_streak = x

print(max_streak)
```

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: [1,1,1,2,2,2,3,3,3,4,4,4,5] for x=13
Frequencies: 1:3, 2:3, 3:3, 4:3, 5:1
freq_set = {1,3}, f1=1, f2=3
f1 == 1 and count_f1 == 1, count_f2 == 4 -> valid, max_streak = 13

Example 4: [3,2,1,1,4,5,1] for x=6
Frequencies: 3:1, 2:1, 1:2, 4:1, 5:1
freq_set = {1,2}, f1=1, f2=2
f2 = f1 + 1 and count_f2 == 1 (color '1' has 2), count_f1 == 4 -> valid

Example 5: [1,1,1,2,2,2] for x=5
Frequencies: 1:3, 2:2
freq_set = {2,3}, f1=2, f2=3
f2 = f1 + 1, count_f2 == 1 (color 1 has 3), count_f1 == 1 (color 2 has 2) -> valid

All examples work!

But what about the single color case? We already handle it separately.

Let me check edge cases.

Example 2: [10,2,5,4,1] -> all different, frequencies: 1:1, 2:1, 4:1, 5:1, 10:1
freq_set = {1}, len=1 -> all same -> invalid
But answer is 5.

Wait, for x=5, we have all colors with frequency 1.
After removing one day, that color disappears and others have frequency 1.
So the remaining colors have frequency 1 - this works!

The condition is: if all colors have frequency 1, and we remove one color, the remaining colors all have frequency 1.

So in this case, after removal, the colors that remain all have frequency 1.

So the valid case is: if all colors have frequency 1 (after removal, remaining colors have frequency 1).

This is a special case of the "f1=1" case? 

No, when all have frequency 1, we have f1=1, and count_f1 = number of colors.

After removing one color, the remaining colors still have frequency 1.

So the condition should be: if all frequencies are 1, then it's valid.

Or more generally: if all non-removed colors have frequency 1.

In the case where all frequencies are 1, after removing any color, the remaining colors have frequency 1 (since they were 1 and unchanged).

So this is valid.

So I need to add this case.

Actually, in my algorithm above, if all frequencies are 1, then freq_set = {1}, and len(freq_set) == 1, which goes to the "all same frequency" case and is skipped.

So I need to handle the case where all frequencies are 1.

OR, I can say: after removing one day, all remaining colors have frequency 1 if initially all had frequency 1.

So the valid cases are:
1. All colors have frequency 1 (after removing one day, remaining colors have frequency 1)
2. One color has frequency f+1, others have frequency f
3. One color has frequency 1, others have frequency f (and we remove the one with frequency 1)

But case 3: if one color has frequency 1, and others have frequency f, after removing that one, others have frequency f - which is not necessarily 1.

I think I'm overthinking.

Actually, let me define what "all ribbon colors have the same number of occurrences" means after removal.

It means every color that appears after removal has the same frequency.

Case A: We remove a color that appears once. That color disappears. All remaining colors have their original frequency.
We need all remaining colors to have the same frequency.

Case B: We remove a color that appears k times. That color now appears k-1 times. All other colors have their original frequency.
We need all colors to have the same frequency f.

So for Case B: we need (original frequency of removed color) - 1 = original frequency of all other colors.

For Case A: we need all remaining colors to have the same frequency.

So Case A: all remaining colors have same frequency f.
Case B: (removed color's frequency) - 1 = f, and all other colors have frequency f.

Case A: if we remove a color with frequency 1, then we need all other colors to have the same frequency f.

Case B: if we remove a color with frequency f+1, then we need all other colors to have frequency f.

So the conditions are:
1. There exists a color with frequency 1, and all other colors have the same frequency f (and we remove that one)
2. There exists a color with frequency f+1, and all other colors have frequency f (and we remove that one)
3. All colors have frequency 1 (so after removal, remaining colors have frequency 1)

For case 3: all have frequency 1. After removing any one, remaining colors have frequency 1. So valid.

For case 1: remove color with frequency 1, others have frequency f. After removal, others have frequency f. So valid.

For case 2: remove color with frequency f+1, others have frequency f. After removal, others have frequency f, and the removed color has frequency f. So all have frequency f. Valid.

So the implementation:

```python
n = int(input())
colors = list(map(int, input().split()))

max_streak = 0

for x in range(1, n + 1):
    freq = {}
    for i in range(x):
        color = colors[i]
        freq[color] = freq.get(color, 0) + 1
    
    freq_values = list(freq.values())
    
    # Case 3: all colors have frequency 1
    if all(f == 1 for f in freq_values):
        max_streak = x
        continue
    
    # Case 1: one color has frequency 1, others have the same frequency f
    freq_set = set(freq_values)
    if len(freq_set) == 2:
        freqs = sorted(freq_set)
        f1, f2 = freqs[0], freqs[1]
        
        # Case: one color has frequency 1, others have frequency f
        if f1 == 1 and freq_values.count(1) == 1:
            count_f2 = freq_values.count(f2)
            if count_f2 == len(freq_values) - 1:
                max_streak = x
        
        # Case: one color has frequency f+1, others have frequency f
        if f2 == f1 + 1 and freq_values.count(f2) == 1:
            count_f1 = freq_values.count(f1)
            if count_f1 == len(freq_values) - 1:
                max_streak = x
    
    # Case 2: all have the same frequency (not 1)
    elif len(freq_set) == 1:
        # All have frequency f
        # After removing one day, one color has f-1, others have f
        # For all to have the same frequency, we need f-1 = f -> impossible
        # So invalid
        pass
    
    # More than 2 frequencies - invalid

print(max_streak)
```

Test with examples:

Example 2: [10,2,5,4,1] for x=5
All have frequency 1 -> all are 1 -> valid, max_streak=5

Example 1: x=13, frequencies: 3,3,3,3,1
freq_set = {1,3}, f1=1, f2=3
f1==1 and count of 1 is 1 -> valid

Example 4: x=6, frequencies: 1,1,1,1,2 (colors 3,2,4,5 have 1, color 1 has 2)
f1=1, f2=2, f2 = f1+1 and count of 2 is 1 -> valid

Example 5: x=5, frequencies: 3,2
f1=2, f2=3, f2=f1+1 and count of 3 is 1 -> valid

Example 3: [10] -> one color, all have frequency 1 -> valid

All work!

But what about a case where all have frequency 2? 
Then after removing one day, one color has frequency 1, others have 2 - not equal. Invalid.

What about all have frequency 1 except one has frequency 2?
For example: [1,1,2] - frequencies: 1:2, 2:1
f1=1, f2=2, f2 = f1 + 1 and count of 2 is 1 -> valid.

After removing color 2 (frequency 1), colors: 1 appears 2 times. Only one color, so all have frequency 2.

Or after removing color 1 (frequency 2), color 1 has frequency 1, color 2 has frequency 1 - both have frequency 1.

Oh! This is a problem.

In [1,1,2], if we remove color 1 (frequency 2), then frequency of color 1 becomes 1, color 2 has frequency 1 - both have frequency 1. Valid.

If we remove color 2 (frequency 1), then color 1 has frequency 2 - only one color, so all have frequency 2. Valid.

So both are valid.

But in my algorithm, for frequencies [2,1]:
freq_set = {1,2}
f1=1, f2=2
- f1==1 and count of 1 is 1 -> valid (remove color with frequency 1)
- f2 == f1+1 and count of 2 is 1 -> valid (remove color with frequency 2)

So both cases are valid, and we will set max_streak = x.

This is correct.

So final code:

```python
n = int(input())
colors = list(map(int, input().split()))

max_streak = 0

for x in range(1, n + 1):
    freq = {}
    for i in range(x):
        color = colors[i]
        freq[color] = freq.get(color, 0) + 1
    
    freq_values = list(freq.values())
    
    # Case 3: all colors have frequency 1
    if all(f == 1 for f in freq_values):
        max_streak = x
        continue
    
    # Case 1: one color has frequency 1, others have the same frequency f
    freq_set = set(freq_values)
    if len(freq_set) == 2:
        freqs = sorted(freq_set)
        f1, f2 = freqs[0], freqs[1]
        
        # Case: one color has frequency 1, others have frequency f
        if f1 == 1:
            count_ones = freq_values.count(1)
            if count_ones == 1:
                count_f2 = freq_values.count(f2)
                if count_f2 == len(freq_values) - 1:
                    max_streak = x
        
        # Case: one color has frequency f+1, others have frequency f
        if f2 == f1 + 1:
            count_f2 = freq_values.count(f2)
            if count_f2 == 1:
                count_f1 = freq_values.count(f1)
                if count_f1 == len(freq_values) - 1:
                    max_streak = x
    
    # Case 2: all have the same frequency (not 1) - invalid
    elif len(freq_set) == 1:
        # Only one frequency, not 1
        pass
    
    # More than 2 frequencies - invalid

print(max_streak)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

This problem is same as the next one, but has smaller constraints.

Shiro's just moved to the new house. She wants to invite all friends of her to the house so they can play monopoly. However, her house is too small, so she can only invite one friend at a time.

For each of the $n$ days since the day Shiro moved to the new house, there will be exactly one cat coming to the Shiro's house. The cat coming in the $i$-th day has a ribbon with color $u_i$. Shiro wants to know the largest number $x$, such that if we consider the streak of the first $x$ days, it is possible to remove exactly one day from this streak so that every ribbon color that has appeared among the remaining $x - 1$ will have the same number of occurrences.

For example, consider the following sequence of $u_i$: $[2, 2, 1, 1, 5, 4, 4, 5]$. Then $x = 7$ makes a streak, since if we remove the leftmost $u_i = 5$, each ribbon color will appear exactly twice in the prefix of $x - 1$ days. Note that $x = 8$ doesn't form a streak, since you must remove exactly one day. 

Since Shiro is just a cat, she is not very good at counting and needs your help finding the longest streak.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $n$ ($1 \leq n \leq 10^5$) — the total number of days.

The second line contains $n$ integers $u_1, u_2, \ldots, u_n$ ($1 \leq u_i \leq 10$) — the colors of the ribbons the cats wear. 


-----Output-----

Print a single integer $x$ — the largest possible streak of days.


-----Examples-----
Input
13
1 1 1 2 2 2 3 3 3 4 4 4 5

Output
13
Input
5
10 2 5 4 1

Output
5
Input
1
10

Output
1
Input
7
3 2 1 1 4 5 1

Output
6
Input
6
1 1 1 2 2 2

Output
5


-----Note-----

In the first example, we can choose the longest streak of $13$ days, since upon removing the last day out of the streak, all of the remaining colors $1$, $2$, $3$, and $4$ will have the same number of occurrences of $3$. Note that the streak can also be $10$ days (by removing the $10$-th day from this streak) but we are interested in the longest streak.

In the fourth example, if we take the streak of the first $6$ days, we can remove the third day from this streak then all of the remaining colors $1$, $2$, $3$, $4$ and $5$ will occur exactly once.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the largest number `x` such that in the first `x` days, we can remove exactly one day so that all remaining ribbon colors have the same frequency.

Key observations:
1. We can only remove exactly one day
2. After removal, all remaining colors must have the same frequency
3. We want the maximum possible `x`

## Analysis of Possible Cases

When we remove one day, the remaining frequencies must all be equal. Let's think about what this means:

**Case 1: All colors appear exactly once in the remaining days**
- This means all colors in the remaining sequence appear exactly once
- If we have `k` different colors, each appears once

**Case 2: All colors appear more than once in the remaining days**
- All colors have the same frequency `f > 1`

## Step-by-step Approach

1. For each prefix length `x` (from 1 to n), check if we can remove exactly one day to make all remaining frequencies equal.

2. For a given prefix of length `x`, we need to:
   - Count frequency of each color
   - Try removing each day (from position 1 to x)
   - Check if after removal, all remaining frequencies are equal

3. The conditions for valid removal:
   - For a color that appears `f` times, if we remove one occurrence of it, it appears `f-1` times
   - For colors that don't appear in the removed position, they remain at their original frequency

4. After removal, all frequencies must be equal.

## Algorithm

For each prefix length `x`, we need to check if there's a day we can remove such that all remaining frequencies are equal.

The frequencies after removing one day will be:
- For colors that weren't removed: original frequency
- For the color of the removed day: original frequency - 1

For all frequencies to be equal, we need:
- All colors that appear in the remaining sequence have the same frequency

This means:
- Either all colors except one have frequency `f`, and the one color has frequency `f-1`
- Or all colors have frequency `f`

## Implementation Strategy

For each prefix length `x`:
1. Count frequencies of all colors in the first `x` days
2. For each possible removal position `i`:
   - Create new frequency count (remove the i-th element)
   - Check if all frequencies are equal
3. If any such removal works, `x` is a valid streak

Since we want the largest `x`, we can check from `x = n` down to `1`, and return the first valid one.

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    u = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Function to check if a prefix can form a valid streak
    def can_form_streak(x):
        if x == 1:
            return True
            
        # Count frequencies of colors in first x days
        freq = {}
        for i in range(x):
            freq[u[i]] = freq.get(u[i], 0) + 1
        
        # Try removing each day
        for remove_idx in range(x):
            # Create frequency count after removing day 'remove_idx'
            new_freq = freq.copy()
            color_to_remove = u[remove_idx]
            
            # Remove one occurrence of the color
            new_freq[color_to_remove] -= 1
            if new_freq[color_to_remove] == 0:
                del new_freq[color_to_remove]
            
            # Check if all remaining frequencies are equal
            if len(new_freq) == 0:
                return True
                
            frequencies = list(new_freq.values())
            if all(f == frequencies[0] for f in frequencies):
                return True
        
        return False
    
    # Start from the largest possible streak and go downwards
    for x in range(n, 0, -1):
        if can_form_streak(x):
            return x
    
    return 1

print(solve())
```

Wait, let me trace through the examples to verify:

Example 1: [1,1,1,2,2,2,3,3,3,4,4,4,5], expected output 13
- For x=13, we need to remove one day such that all remaining have same frequency
- If we remove the last 5, we have 1:3, 2:3, 3:3, 4:3 - all have frequency 3 ✓

Example 4: [3,2,1,1,4,5,1], expected output 6
- For x=6: [3,2,1,1,4,5]
- If we remove position 2 (value 2): colors 3:1, 1:2, 4:1, 5:1 → not equal
- If we remove position 3 (value 1): colors 3:1, 2:1, 1:1, 4:1, 5:1 → all 1 ✓

The algorithm seems correct. Let me optimize it slightly:

```python

        return True

        print(x)
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the largest number `x` such that in the first `x` days, we can remove exactly one day so that all remaining ribbon colors appear the same number of times.

Key observations:
1. We can only remove exactly one day from the streak
2. After removal, all remaining colors must have the same frequency
3. We want the maximum possible `x`

## Analysis

Let's think about when we can remove one day to make all colors have the same frequency:

**Case 1: All colors have the same frequency**
- If we have k colors, each appearing f times, then we can remove one occurrence of any color, and we'll have k colors each appearing f-1 times.
- This works if we can remove one day.

**Case 2: Most colors have frequency f, one color has frequency f+1**
- If we remove one occurrence of the color that appears f+1 times, we get all colors appearing f times.

**Case 3: One color appears once, others appear f times**
- We can remove that single occurrence.

**Case 4: Two colors have frequency f+1, others have frequency f**
- This won't work because removing one occurrence won't balance everything.

## Approach

For each possible streak length x from 1 to n, we need to check if we can remove exactly one day to make all remaining colors have the same frequency.

But we want the maximum x. So we can try to check for each x from n down to 1, and return the first valid x.

However, we can be smarter:

For a streak of length x to work:
- Count the frequency of each color
- If all frequencies are equal (say f), then we can remove one occurrence of any color (except when f=1, we might not have enough)
- If one frequency is different from others (say freq[i] = f+1, others = f), then we can remove one occurrence of that color and get all frequencies = f

But we need to be careful about edge cases.

## Step-by-step solution

1. For each x from n down to 1:
   - Take the first x days
   - Count frequencies of each color
   - Check if we can remove one day to make all frequencies equal
   - Return x if possible

2. To check if we can remove one day:
   - If all frequencies are equal: possible (except when all frequencies are 1 and x=1, but that works)
   - If exactly one frequency is different from others: possible
   - Otherwise: not possible

Actually, let me reconsider. The frequencies can be:
- All equal: valid
- One frequency is f+1, others are f: valid (remove one from the f+1 group)
- One frequency is 1, others are f: valid (remove the single one)

Wait, let me think more systematically:

For frequencies to become equal after removing one day:
- Let the frequencies be: f1, f2, ..., fk
- After removing one occurrence, we need all to be equal

So we need to check:
1. All fi = f for some f
2. All fi = f except one fi = f+1

Also need to consider the case where one fi = 1 and others are f.

Actually, the only valid cases are:
- All frequencies are equal: remove any day
- All frequencies except one are equal to f, one is equal to f+1: remove one from the f+1 frequency

But we can also have the case where one frequency is 1 and others are f.

Actually, if one frequency is 1 and others are f, removing that 1 makes all f.

So the valid cases are:
- All frequencies equal to f
- All frequencies equal to f except one equal to f+1  
- All frequencies equal to f except one equal to 1

Wait, no - if one is 1 and others are f, removing that 1 makes all f.

So the valid cases are:
1. All frequencies equal to f
2. All frequencies equal to f except one equal to f+1
3. All frequencies equal to f except one equal to 1

But cases 2 and 3 both have "one frequency different".

Actually, in case 3, if one color appears once and others appear f times, then after removing that one, all appear f times.

So we need to check if we can make all frequencies equal.

## Implementation

I'll iterate from x=n down to 1 and check each prefix.

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    colors = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Try each streak length from n down to 1
    for x in range(n, 0, -1):
        # Count frequencies in first x days
        freq = {}
        for i in range(x):
            color = colors[i]
            freq[color] = freq.get(color, 0) + 1
        
        # Get frequency counts
        freqs = list(freq.values())
        freqs.sort()
        
        # Check if we can remove exactly one day to make all frequencies equal
        # Case 1: All frequencies are equal
        if len(freqs) == 1:
            # Only one distinct color, we can remove one occurrence
            # After removal, all remain 0, so effectively no colors left
            # But we need at least one color with positive frequency
            # If x==1, removing the only day leaves empty set - possible?
            # The problem says "every ribbon color that has appeared" - if none, it's vacuously true
            # But let's see the examples
            # For x=1, we remove that day, no colors left - vacuously all colors have same frequency (0)
            # So this should be valid
            # Actually, if there's only one color and we remove it, there are no colors left
            # The condition is satisfied (vacuously)
            # So x=1 is valid
            return x
        
        # Case 2: All frequencies equal
        if all(f == freqs[0] for f in freqs):
            return x
        
        # Case 3: One frequency is different (either f+1 or 1)
        # Count how many frequencies are equal to freqs[0]
        count_freqs = {}
        for f in freqs:
            count_freqs[f] = count_freqs.get(f, 0) + 1
        
        # Check if exactly one frequency is different
        different_count = 0
        different_value = None
        
        for f, cnt in count_freqs.items():
            if f != freqs[0]:
                if cnt == 1:
                    # One frequency different, check if it's f+1 or 1
                    if f == freqs[0] + 1:
                        # Remove one from this color
                        # After removal, all will be freqs[0]
                        return x
                    elif f == 1:
                        # Remove this single occurrence
                        # After removal, all others will be freqs[0]
                        return x
                else:
                    # Multiple frequencies different - not valid
                    break
        else:
            # If we reached here, no different frequency found
            continue
        
        # Actually, I need to check more systematically
        # Find the mode frequency
        mode = freqs[0]
        
        # Count how many colors have frequency mode
        mode_count = 0
        for f in freqs:
            if f == mode:
                mode_count += 1
        
        # For the remaining colors
        remaining_count = len(freqs) - mode_count
        
        # If remaining_count == 1, check the different frequency
        if remaining_count == 1:
            different_f = None
            for f in freqs:
                if f != mode:
                    different_f = f
                    break
            
            # Check if we can remove one from different_f
            if different_f == mode + 1 or different_f == 1:
                return x
        
        # If no valid case found, continue to smaller x
        pass
    
    # If no x found, return 1 (shouldn't happen)
    return 1

print(solve())
```

Wait, I'm overcomplicating. Let me simplify:

For a streak of length x to work:
- Count frequencies of each color in first x days
- If all frequencies are equal, we can remove any one day (removes one occurrence, all become f-1)
- If all frequencies are equal to f except one frequency is f+1, we can remove one occurrence from that color
- If all frequencies are equal to f except one frequency is 1, we can remove that single occurrence

So I need to:
1. Count frequencies
2. Find the frequencies
3. Check if we can have at most one frequency different from the mode
4. And that different frequency must be either mode+1 or 1

```python
n = int(input())
colors = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Try each streak length from n down to 1
for x in range(n, 0, -1):
    # Count frequencies in first x days
    freq = {}
    for i in range(x):
        color = colors[i]
        freq[color] = freq.get(color, 0) + 1
    
    freqs = list(freq.values())
    freqs.sort()
    
    # If only one color, we can remove one occurrence
    if len(freqs) == 1:
        # Removing one occurrence from the only color
        # Now 0 occurrences of that color
        # Vacuously all colors have same frequency (0)
        # So this should be valid
        print(x)
        exit()
    
    # Check all frequencies are equal
    if all(f == freqs[0] for f in freqs):
        print(x)
        exit()
    
    # Check if exactly one frequency is different
    # Count frequencies of each frequency value
    freq_count = {}
    for f in freqs:
        freq_count[f] = freq_count.get(f, 0) + 1
    
    # Find the most common frequency (mode)
    mode = freqs[0]
    
    # Count how many frequencies have the mode value
    mode_count = 0
    for f in freqs:
        if f == mode:
            mode_count += 1
    
    # The remaining frequencies (not mode)
    remaining_freqs = []
    for f in freqs:
        if f != mode:
            remaining_freqs.append(f)
    
    # If there's exactly one remaining frequency value
    if len(remaining_freqs) == 1:
        different_freq = remaining_freqs[0]
        
        # Check if we can remove one occurrence to balance
        # Case 1: different_freq == mode + 1
        # Case 2: different_freq == 1
        if different_freq == mode + 1 or different_freq == 1:
            print(x)
            exit()
    
    # If we get here, no valid removal found for this x
    pass

# Should not reach here
print(1)
```

So condition: after removing one day, all frequencies should be equal.

This happens when:

In case 2, if we have frequencies [f, f, ..., f, f+1], removing one from the f+1 color makes all f.
